--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'll analyze the mission and design FSMs for it.

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score increases when robots are OUTSIDE the target region (blue-walled strip with BLACK floor)
- Both strips have BLACK floors; the middle is GRAY
- Target: get robots INTO the blue-walled strip (black floor near blue walls)
- Key challenge: distinguish the two black strips - blue walls vs green walls

**Strategy:**
- Robots need to move toward blue-colored walls (GoToColor with blue)
- When on black floor near blue walls = good; when on gray = navigate toward blue
- Use floor color (black/gray) and wall color perception to guide robots
- GoToColor B=8 with clr=3 (blue) should pull robots toward blue walls
- GoAwayColor B=9 with clr=2 (green) should push robots away from green walls
- Floor BLACK = possibly in target, GRAY = definitely not in target

**State Design:**
- State 0: GoToColor (blue, clr=3) - seek blue walls
- State 1: Exploration - when can't see blue, explore to find it
- Transitions: seeing blue → go to it; on black floor → stay/reinforce; on gray → explore

Let me design 14 machines with varied strategies:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
**Reasoning:**

I made an error - LED colors are limited to {0, 4, 5, 6} (0=off, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan). Blue (3) is not valid for `--cle`. I'll fix all LED color references.

**Core strategy remains:**
- GoToColor with clr=3 (blue) moves robots toward blue walls (target strip)
- GoAwayColor with clr=2 (green) pushes away from wrong strip
- Floor GRAY = in middle, transition to seeking blue walls
- Floor BLACK = possibly in target or wrong strip
- Exploration/zigzag when no blue walls visible

I'll use LED color 6 (cyan) to mark robots heading to blue, 4 (yellow) for exploring, 0 for stopped/other states.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
The error says `--cle` with value `3` is still appearing. Looking carefully, the issue is that `--clr0 3` is being confused — but wait, `--clr` is the color for GoToColor behavior, not the LED color. Let me re-read...

The error is `--cle0` with value `3`. But I used `--cle0 6` in my last answers. Oh wait — could it be that `--clr0 3` in `--s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 3` is being misread as LED? No, `--clr` is separate from `--cle`.

Actually, re-reading: the errors say `--cle1` with value `3`. In FSM line 4: `--s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 3 --cle1 6` — that looks fine. But wait, maybe the simulator is reading `--clr1 3` and treating it as `--cle1 3`? Or perhaps some FSMs I submitted still had `--cle 3` from copy errors.

Let me be very careful: I'll avoid `--clr 3` entirely and use a different approach — GoAwayColor from green (clr=2) instead of GoToColor blue (clr=3), combined with exploration.

**Revised strategy:** Use GoAwayColor green (clr=2) to escape green walls, Exploration to navigate, floor-color conditions to detect strips.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
The error persists saying `--cle` with value `3`. But I haven't been writing `--cle 3` explicitly. Let me look very carefully...

In my FSMs I write things like `--clr0 3` (color for GoToColor behavior) and `--cle0 6` (LED color). Could the simulator be parsing `--clr0 3` as `--cle`? No, they're different flags.

Wait — I see it now! In FSM line 4 of round 3: `--s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 3 --cle1 6` — this is fine. But in round 2's original submissions I had `--cle0 3` by mistake (using blue=3 as LED color).

The issue might be that the **error log is showing cumulative/cached errors from round 2** that I haven't fully cleared. Let me ensure I have absolutely NO `--clr` value of anything that could be misread, and double-check every single `--cle` parameter.

Actually the safest fix: **avoid GoToColor with clr=3 entirely**. Use only GoAwayColor from green (clr=2), and other behaviors that don't use clr=3.

**Strategy:** GoAwayColor green (clr=2) + Exploration + floor sensing. Robots flee green walls and end up in blue-walled strip.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
The error keeps saying `--cle` with value `3` but I'm not writing that explicitly. Let me look at this very carefully.

The error message says `--cle0` and `--cle1` with value `3`. In my FSMs I have things like `--clr0 2` and `--cle0 6`. 

Wait - could `--clr` be getting parsed as `--cle`? The flags `--clr` and `--cle` share the prefix `--cl`. Maybe the parser is doing prefix matching and `--clr` is matching `--cle`? But they differ at position 4 (r vs e).

Actually - I wonder if the issue is that in SOME of my FSMs I accidentally wrote `--clr0 3` somewhere, and the simulator interprets `--clr` as setting LED color? Let me re-read the spec: `--clr<I>` is for GoToColor/GoAwayColor behavior color, `--cle<I>` is LED color.

The ONLY explanation: the error report is cumulative from round 2 where I genuinely had `--cle0 3`. Let me just make sure every single `--cle` value is strictly in {0,4,5,6} and move on. I'll avoid any ambiguity by only using `--cle 0`, `--cle 4`, `--cle 5`, or `--cle 6`.

**Reasoning:** Same GoAwayColor-green strategy. Robots flee green walls, explore gray middle, settle in blue-walled black strip. I'll keep successful topologies and explore variations.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
The error keeps repeating even though I'm not writing `--cle 3` explicitly. I need to investigate what's actually causing this. 

Looking very carefully: could `--clr0 2` be parsed as `--cle` something? The flags are `--clr` vs `--cle` - they differ. BUT wait - what if the argument parser sees `--cl` as a prefix and matches greedily? If `--clr` isn't recognized but `--cle` is, could `--clr` fall through as `--cle`?

Actually re-reading the spec: GoToColor requires `--clr<I>` and GoAwayColor requires `--clr<I>`. These ARE valid parameters. So `--clr` should be recognized.

**New hypothesis:** The error log is simply being carried forward from round 2 as a persistent warning, and my machines are actually running fine. The scores just haven't been shared with me, so I can't tell which machines performed best. I'll continue with the same strategy but diversify more aggressively since I have no score feedback to exploit.

Let me try completely different approaches too - pure exploration, attraction-based, spiral-based - to get more signal.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
The persistent error about `--cle` value `3` is very puzzling since I'm not writing it. Let me look at this from a completely different angle.

Could it be that `--clr0 2` is being parsed as `--cle0` with value... no. Wait - what if the issue is with `--n0 0` in the 1-state FSM? That might cause a parse error that shifts subsequent token parsing.

Actually, let me re-read: `--n<I> T` requires T in [1..4]. So `--n0 0` is INVALID! That was my 1-state FSM `--nstates 1 --s0 9 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 2 --cle0 0 --n0 0` - the `--n0 0` might crash the parser and cause subsequent parameters to be misread!

But that still doesn't explain `--cle 3`. 

My real suspicion now: the error log IS from round 2 and keeps being shown because it's a session-level log, not per-round. My actual machines are running fine but I have no score data to work with.

I'll keep the same strategy, fix the 1-state FSM issue, and diversify further.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
The error persists across all rounds. Since I'm not writing `--cle 3` explicitly, and the error keeps appearing, I must be triggering it somehow indirectly. 

Let me think about what could cause `--cle` to receive value `3`:
- `--clr0 2` → clr≠cle, shouldn't conflict
- But what about `--clr1 2`? Could `--clr` be an unrecognized flag causing the parser to skip it and then read the next token `2` for some other parameter, and then `--cle1` gets `3` from somewhere?

Actually - what if `--clr` is NOT a valid parameter name and the simulator doesn't recognize it? Looking at the spec again: GoAwayColor "requires `--vel<I>` fixed value 1.0" and "requires `--clr<I>`". So `--clr` IS specified. But maybe the actual simulator uses a different flag name?

Let me try completely avoiding `--clr` by not using behaviors 8 or 9 at all. I'll use only: Exploration (B=0), Stop (B=1), Attraction (B=4), Repulsion (B=5), Spiral (B=6), FlockCenter (B=10), ZigZag (B=11) - none of which require `--clr`.

For navigation: use floor color (black=target strip or wrong strip, gray=middle) and wall color perception (C=7 with color IDs for blue/green walls) without GoToColor/GoAwayColor.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
The error persists. Since I completely avoided `--clr` in round 9 and still get this error, it must be coming from somewhere else entirely. Let me look at color ID `5` in condition C=7: `--l0x0 5` means "perceive magenta". Could `5` somehow be interpreted as a `--cle` value of `3`? That seems impossible.

Wait - I just realized: the error says `--cle0` with value `3` AND `--cle1` with value `3`. These are BOTH in the same round. What if this is a **cached/stale error from round 2** that keeps being displayed regardless? The system might just be showing historical errors. My machines may actually be running fine.

Since I have no score data to guide exploitation, I'll continue with the best logical strategy (robots detect blue-wall-colored objects via C=7 with color `6`=cyan or other wall colors, use floor detection) while maximizing diversity.

Actually - re-reading the problem: walls are BLUE or GREEN. The color IDs: looking at `--clr` colors {1,2,3,4,5,6} and `--l` colors {1,2,3,4,5,6}. Blue=3, Green=2. So C=7 with `--l 3` detects blue walls, `--l 2` detects green walls. I'll use these without any `--clr` parameter (no B=8 or B=9).

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
